32. Before considering the above submissions, it is apt to refer ,0"
,,
object of the Telangana Tenancy and Agricultural Lands Act, 1950;ii1
formerly known as the Andhra Pradesh (Telangana Ar5a) Tenancy
and Agricultural tqnds Act, 1950. This Act was enunciated to
regulate the relationship between the landlords and tenants, deal
with the rights in or over the lands, land tenures, collection of rents,
transfer and alienation of agricultural land. The State legislature with
a taudable object as part of ggrarian reforms enacted the Tenancy
Act, providing various provisions for transfer, alienation of
agricultural land and for other altied purposes. tlefore discussing

il Findinqs of the RDO:
The RDO held that the petitioners have been in possession of
the subject lands as tenants since 1950 and therefore, they have
acquired vested tenancy rights. These rights, being pre-existing,
cannot be divested merely due to the issuance of G.O.Ms.No. f 83
dated 24.O3.2OO0. Accordingly, the issue was decided in favour of the
petitioners.
iil Findinqs of the Joint Collector:
The Joint Collector opined that once the land is notified for
residential use under G.O.Ms.No.183, it ceases to be agricultural
land under the Tenancy Act., 1950. In such a case, Section 102(e)
bars the application of the Tenancy Act, and the authorities under
the Act lose jurisdiction. Hence , the RDO's conclusion was held to be
incorrect and was set aside.

a) 'l'he Tenancy Act, 1950 ,i*" "rrIcld ,o ,"grlate the relationship
il*: ' .
'. betwee:r landowners and tenants. This Act aimed at agrarian reform,
.i. ,,.
was de,signed to establish a socialistic pattern in society and governs
the trarrsfer, alienation, and possesiior, of agricultural lands. It falls
under tlntry 18 of List II, and is well within the legislative competence
of the Sitate. Under tJ:e provisions of the Act, if a landowner seeks to
regain Jrossession of land.from tenant or protected tenant, they must
follow the procedure prescribed. Any question regarding whether a
person is a protected tenant or ttre surrender of tenancy rights can
only be adjudicated under the provisions of the Act. The relationship
<rf landklrd and tenant is a sfne quatwn for invoking the provisions of
this legislation. Section 5 of the Act provides that a person lzru'tully
cultivating the land belortgrng to another shall bc dccmccl to bc a
tcn.int, unless the land is being personally cultivate(l by thc
lzrnclholcler. Prior to the ambndment of Section 19 (viric Arnendment
Act No.3 of 1954, Rrblished in Andhra Pradesh ()azette
fi.xtrerordinary No.29, dated 4.2.19541, the surrender o{' tcnancy could
tre rrral. After the amendment to Section l9 r.r,.c.l'. 04.O2.1954,
surrcnder must be in writing and admitted beforc ttrc 'lzr]rsildar. In
the arbserrce of such surender, the tenant continucs to h:rvc right to
rccover the possession. F\rrther; Section 33 of the Act clarifies that

except for exceptions curved outiin Section 3O(U of the Act, nothing

in the Tenanry Act, shall be constnred to limit or abridge the right or

that tenants' accrued rights shall not be defeated by subsequent laws
or enactments.

(
9l CVBR,J
CRP No.5279D016 &Batch
does not affect the rights of thc protected tqng..nts,, F\rrther, Section
38E of the Tenancy Act starts wittr non-obstante clause giving
overriding effect of all the provisions of the Act and vesting of the
rights in favour of the Protected Tenants, from the date of notilication
i.e, 01.()1.1973 subject to fulfilling the conditions under sub-section
(7) of St:ction 38 of the Tenanpy Act. Admittedly, no notification was
issued trnder Section LO2 (el of the Tenancy Act divesting the accrued
rights under Sections 34 and 37A of the Tenancy Act. Further, a
conjoint reading o[ Section 33 r/w $ection 104 of t]re Tenancy Act,
would amply establish the intention of the legislature that the rights
conferred under the Act, shall be construed to li.mit or abridge the
rights o:- privileges of any tenant. A summary of the above analysis
would mpl)' ttrat G.O.Ms.No.183 dated 28.O4.2OOO is not a
notification issued under Section 1O2(e) of Tenancy Act. [t is to be
seen thitt ()nce rights have been created under the statute, suctr
rights cz:nr1o[ bc clenuded without following the procedure containcd
unclcr ttrc 'lcnancy Act. Therefore, it can be safety concluded that
G.O.Ms.ltlo.l83 dated 28.O4.2OOO is not applicable to the subjecr
lands on tw() folds. Firstly, the tenancy proceedings are pending from
the year l9B0 onwards before this Court vide W.P.No.459O of 1980,
W.P.No.4059 of 1982 and WA No.1420 of L987 etc., and before thc
authoriti:s trnder the Tenancy Act and the issuance of subsequent
norificerri:rn vidc G.O.Ms.No. iSS dated 28.04.2OOO cannor.

CRP No.527912016 & Batch
retrospectively nultify the rights. S.$ondly, ''the declaration filed by
i:
the Kastopa Co,rporation under the provisions of CoAII Act, 1973 was
computed and the possession of surplus lands was taken by the
State on l3.l0. 1976, after the UDA Act, Lg75 and UI,C Act, 19T6
came into force. The purchasers from Kaslopa Corporation
(respondents in revision cases) failed to establish that there was
conversion order issued by the Collector for appropriating the
agricultural land for non-agricultural purpgse under Rule TO of
Telangana Land Revenue Rules, 1951 nor placed any record of
payment of Non-Agricultural Assessment Ta:r under the provisions of
NALA Act, 1963. The above facts would maniiest that the nature o[
lands are agriculture and protected tenarrcy rights were crystallized
by thc ycar 1958 under Section 37-A of the TgnancyAct. Such vesred
tenancy rights cannot be divested by a subsequent reclassilication
undcr UDA Act, 1975. Therefore, G.O.Ms.No.183 dated 24.O3.2OOO
does n()t rctrospectively extinguish protected tenancy rights. The
findings ol'rhc .loint Collector to the contrar5r are set aside.

to determine who is the protected teaant under Rule 14 read

il Findtrss of the RDO:
'Ihe RDO stated that the report dated 27.07.1981 was upheld
in W.F'.No.4059 of L982 and- confirmed in W.A.No.l42O of 1987. As
no fur{her appeal was filed, the findings have attained finality and are
bindin;3 on the Government. Therefore, the matter cannot be
reopened.
iil lrindinqs qf the Joiot Collector:
T'he Joint Collector clarified that the RDO conducted the
enquir5' only on the directions of in W.P.No.45gO/ 1980, and not as'
an auttrorit5r under the Tenancy Act. The report is thus only a piece of
evidence and not a statr.rtory order. Consequently, the RDO lacked
jurisdiction under Rule 14 or 26 to determine protected tenancy, and
the RDO's lindings were set aside.

other larv in force. This indicates t]rat the RDO, as a Supcrior ()lficcr,

0 ln addition to the a-bove findings, it is important to emphasize
that when a judicial forum like the High Court, in exercise of its
powers under Article 226 of the Constitution of India, specificall5r
calls fcr a fact-finding report from the designated oflicer, not below
the rarLk of Revenue Divisional Oflicer and the report submitted in
complizrnce with such judicial directions becomes part and parcel of
the judicial proceedings. Such a report carries not only evidentiary
value but also sancti$r, particularly when the same is accepted and
atrainecl finality. An administrative or quasi-judicial authority like the
Joint C:ollector cannot sit over an appellate authority over the
judicially affirmed factual report which was upheld by this Court'
Furtherrnore, Rule 14 and Rule 26 of the Hyderabad Tenancy and
Agricultural Land Rules, 1950, prescribe the procedure for
determination of protected tenancy status and for recording entries in
the tenancy register. While these functions are ordinarily carried out

by the Tahsildar, tJre enabling provisitins of Sections 88 and 89 of the

. under the Telangana Lard Revenue Ac! make it clear that superior \-
off-rcers like the RDO or Collector exercising power under the Rules,
1950, are not divested of jurisdiction, p.4i"rrt".ly when exercising
{
delegated functions or acting upon specific judicial directions.
g) The reasoning of the Joint collector in treating the RDo's
report as lacking jurisdiction or evidentiary value is untenable. Thus
it is held that the RDO acted within jurisdiction in accordance with
law and the findings recorde&therein are uinaing subsequently in all
proceedings emanated from the Tenancy Act. In view o[ the above
discussion, the findings of the Joint Collector on this issue are scr
asidc.

iiil Eindines of this Court
a) It is the case o[ petitioners that the respondents/purchasers
being third-part5r purchascrs have no legs to stand to object the

d) 'lhe Legislature enacted the Andhra Pradesh (Telangana Area)
Tenancy and Agricultural Lands (Validation) Act, 1961 retrospectively
v.alidating the Tenancy Act, 1950 together with the arnendnients
thereto. This validating Act of t96l received the assent of. ttre
Presiderrt and was placcd in thc gttr sshsclule of the Constihrtion. The
Governrnent issued G.O.Ms.No.3 clatcd Ol-O l-1g73 notiffing alt the
Districtsr in Telangana under Section 38E for the purpose of stahrtory
transfer of ownership to prcltcctccl tcnants. Section 97 conferred rule
making power upon ttrc (iovernmcnt and Section '99 barred the
jurisdiction of a civil Court t() scttle or decide or deal-with any
question which is required to be scttlccl by the Tahsitdar, TribUnal or
Collector or Board o[ Revenue or Government. Section 38 enables a
protectecl tenant to purchase Ltre land holders interest in the'land
held by t:he former as a protected tcnant. Sub-sections (2) to (8] of

114 cvB&J
CRP No.5279l2016 & Batdr
Section 38 prescribe the procedure for the exercise of the tight
conferred upon the protected tenant to purchase the land holders
interest in the.land. The restrictions imposed unddr sub-section (7)
of Section 38 will not apply to sale by the landholder to a protected
tenant by mutual consent, provided the price payable therefor is
agreed to between them. Section 38A makes the restrictions imposed
under Section 38 (7) inapplicable to cases of sale by mutual consent
between the land holder and the protected tenant. Under Section
38E, the Government is empowerert to declare, by notification in the
Gazntte in respect of any area and from sttch date as may be specified
therein, that the ownership of all lands, held by the protected tenants
which they are entitled to purchase from their land holders, stand
transferred to and vest in the protected tenants. In respect of the land
held by the protected tenant, the land owner can relinquish his right
in favour of the tenant by executing sale certificate under Section
38A.
e) In the cases on hand, this Court upon examination of the
entire record finds that the respondents in these revisions are third-party purchasers claiming through registered sale deeds allegedly
executed by the landholder and certain individuals stated to be
protected tenants. However, no evidence has been placed on record to
establish that the said vendors had lawful authority to alienate the
o
lands. Moreover, the transactions are l3y-95 derrelopments and

and 318-E of Tenancy Act are confined to the statutory relationshin
between the landholder and the protected tenant. The respondents
herein have no loczrs standi under the Tenancy Act to object statutory
process. The Tenancy authorities cannot adjudicate Llpon the validity
of sale deeds or deterrnine title or possession disputes arising out of
private' conveyances. Further, the declarant /land owner has not
denied the existence of tenants over the lands for their entitlement of
tenar-rqy certificates. Once they are entitled for issuanqe gf tenancy
cgrtificrrtes, on the consent of the owner/declarant, it is immaterial
whether the certilicates issued are under Section 38E or Section 38A
of the l\ct. Both the provisions confer ownership to ttre tenants and
tJ:e saree is not incongruous with the object of the Tenancy Act.
Therefor:e, the petitioners are entitled for issuance of certificates
under Sections 38E and 38A of the Act.

fil Findines of the Joint Ccllector:
r The Joint collector found that the protected tenants had
voluntarily surrendered their tenancy rights during 196566, and the
same w€.s accepted and finalized by the Land Reforms Tribunal in its
order dated o9.r2.l9zs. The petitioners did not challenge the finality
of the said orders. consequently, the lands vested in t]:e Government,
and t.l-e petitioners are estopped from asserting tenancy rights. The
Joint Collector clarilied that Section 38-E resuits in statutory transfer
of ownership by operation of law, while secrion 3g-A is contractual.
.since the two are mutually excrusive, once rights are acquired gnder
Section 38-E, no certificate can be granted under Section 3g-A for the

ifil D'indings of this Court
a) upon a comprehensi,e analysis of the pleadings, docurnents,
and the legal flramework govt-.rning protected tenancy under the
Tenancy Act, this court fincls rhar rhc rights of the petitioners over
the lands situated in Survcy Nos.5 t, s2, and S3(part) of Gachibowli
village were not exringuished b.y. rtre order dared og.l2.lgzs passed
by the Land Reforms Tribunal unclcr the coAH Act, 1973. Although
the LRT's order has attaincd tinality under the ceiting Act, it cannot
override the statutory rights vcstcd in protected tenants under the
ll8

lt9 CVB&J
CRP No.5279DAl6 &Batch
Tenancy Act, 1950. This Court is o{. the view that the proceedings
beforr: the Land Reforms Tribunal. primarily dealt with the holding of
the declarant, M/s. I(astopa Corporation, and the declaration of
surpltrs lands under ifie Ceiling Act. There is no evidence of any
adjudrcation made therein regarding valid surrender by the
petitioners in accordance with Section 19 of the Tenancy Act. In [act,
the RDO in his detailed enquiry report categorically found thar no
valid s -rrrender had taken place in respect of Survey Nos. 51, 52, and
53. While surrender proceedings of the year 1965-66 may pertain ro
other survey numbers like 37, 40, 42,43, and 47, there is no record
of surrt:nder under Section 19 concerning the subject lands. As per
the statutory scheme, for a surrender to be valid under Section lg, it
must be in writing, voluntafily made, and duly accepted by the
Tahsildzrr. The RDO's report clearly states.that no such procedure
rvas [<rll':wed in respect of Survey Nos.51 to 53. Furthernrore, the
rc'p()rt acknowledges that the petitioners or their predecessors-in-irrtt'rcst were declared protected tenants trnder Section 37-A of thc
Tcnancy Act in the year 1958 and these entries continuc to stand
unlt:ss letwfully altered through p{oceedings under the Act. Even
thougtr proceedings under the Ceiling Act have concluded and the
larrcl ardnreasuring Ac.lsV.l7 guntas in different survey numbers
stcrod vesl.ed in the State, tl.e lands in S'urvey Nos. 51, 52, ancl part of
53 rernained unaffected, as no valid surrender or relinquishment of
q
eo*

120 cvBR J
CRP No.527912016 & Barch
protected tenancy rights was proved in respect of these {ands.. The
conclusio. dt"*t'by the Joint Collector that the petitioners' tenancy
rights stood extinguished due to the finaligr of the LRT,s order is not
sustainable. It is also relevant to note that under Sections 33 and
104 of the Tenancy Act, read with section 2g ofthe coAH Act, 1923,
the Tenancy Act enjoys an overriding effect in respect of matters
enumerated under List II of the Seventh Schedule of the Constitution
of India. Thus, in matters concerning tenancy, including surrender,
the Tenancy Act would prevail. A careful reading of sections 33, 34,
and 38-E makes it evident that ownership rights under section 3g-E
would vest in the protected tenant on the notified date, even if the
tenant was not in physical possession, provided there was no valid
surrender under Section 19 and the tenant had not lost status under
sections 32 or 44. rn the present cases, there is no proof of such loss
or disqualification. Therefore, this court holds that the tenancy rights
of the petitioners over the lands in survey Nos. 5 r, s2, and 53 are
intact and not extinguished by the LRT proceedings or orders passed
under the Ceiling Act. Their rights are traceable independently to the
Tenancy Act and are entitled to protection thereunder. Accordingly,
these issues are answered in favour of the revision petitioners/
tenants.

d) From the above decisions, it is clear that section 3g-D of the
Tenancy Act imposes a statutory embargo on the landholder from
alienating tenanted land to anyone other than the protected tenant.
The right of first purchase is vested exclusively in the protected
tenant, and any alienation contrar5r to this mandate .is void and
unenforceable in law. Consequently, any interest created in favour of
third parties prior to the exercise of the protected tenants' statutory
rights under section 38-D cannot override or extinguish such
tenancy rights. In the instant cases, the sale-deeds executed by
M/s.Kastopa corporation in favour of the rlsnoqdents during rhe
pendency of tenancy proceedings and, prior to the conclusion of
ownership rights under Secrion 38-E/38-A *T in violation of
Section 38-D. The purchasers thus l,acked thc legal stand to asser.t
any claim or title over the lands so conveyed, since such transfers
were contrary to the statutory protection afforded to the tenants. The
tenancy rights, even after the demise of the protected tenant, devolve
upon their legal heirs and remain operative until extinguished in

n
137 CVBR.J
CRP No. 5279 fzo I 6 &. Batch
accordance with law. Mere physical occupation or title claims by third
parties, either prior to or post such grant, are not legally sustainable
unless the surrender of tenancy rights has been validly effected
under Section 19 of the Act. Furthermore, any third-party rights
created pending final adjudication of tenancy claims cannot defeat
the clainr of a protected tenant unless there is a clear case of consent,
waiver, or estoppel. The law is settled that protected tenants' rights
are not clefeated by delay alone, and the mere passage of time does
not extinguish them. Where landholders have consciously excluded
certain lemds frorn ceiling declarations, they are estopped from later
denying the rights of protected tenants. The issuance of certificates
under Se:ction .38-E/3S-A may validly follow even without fresh
enquiry if the claim of protected tenancy was earlier admitted or
acknowlerlged, including for the purpose o[ exclusion under land
ceiling lanvs. These legal propositions reinforce the conclusion that
any sale or creation of interest in favour o[ respondents during
pendency of tenancy adjudication proceedings and before the
issuance of certilicates under Section 38-E, is not binding on the
protected tenants or their Successors. Therefore, such sale deeds
have no overriding effect on the tenancy rights of the petitioners,
which stoo,C protected under law.

138 . CVBR,J
CRP No.5279mrc & Batch
4I. This Court has carefully considered the material placed on
record, the submissions of the learned counsel for both sides; and the
legal framework governing the rights of protected tenants under the
Tenancy Act. The undisputed facts reveal that the petitioners were
claiming rights under Section 38-E/38-A of the Tenancy Act over an
extent of Acs.36.25 gts in Survey Nos.51, 52, and 53 (part) situated
at Gachibowli Village. These lands have consistently been recorded as
agricultural in nature, and the protected tenanry rights claimed over
them stood crystallized long before Government Orders were issued
under the Urban Areas (Development) Act, L975, seeking to alter the
land use or reclassify the region. In W.P.No.459O of 198O, this Court
categorically recognized the existence of protected tenants and
directed a fact-linding exercise through the District Collector or an
officer not below the rank of Revenue Divisional Officer (RDO)/Deputy
Collector. Pursuant to the said direction, the RDO submitted a report
dated 27.O7.1981 confirming the tenanry claims. The authenticity of
the report was not disputed before the Division Bench and the same
has attained l-rnality and it cannot be discarded, particularly when it
was never challenged by the declarant Mls. Kastopa Corporation, or
the subsequent purchasers. The RDO, in his subsequent order dated
15.1O.2O13, thoroughly examined the certified extracts of Tenancy
Register and report dated 27.07-1981 and concluded that thc
I
petitioners were entitled to be treated as protected tenants eligible for

44- From the above note file, it is evident that the impugned order
was pasised at the time when the Joint Collector was oflicially on
leave, which raises serious doubt regarding the manner of disposal
and the procedural propriety of the order-making process.
45. The Division Bench while disposing of CRP No.6708 of 2OO3
and batc:h specifically directed the RDO to examine the claims of
tenancy rights over the lands in Sy.Nos.Sl, 52, and S3(Part) to an
extent of Ac.36.25 gts and the said order has been confirmed by the
Hon'lcle llpex Court vide Civil Appeal No.3054 of 2006 and batch.
Thus, the jurisdiction of tJ:e RDO was confined strictly to an extent of
,.ri:' j,-j::'
Ac.36.25 gts in compliance with the orders of the Division Bench.
However, the record discloses that instead of conlining his
examinatirn to the said extent, the RDO held that the tenants were
entitled to Ac.42.24 g[s. Once the orders of the Division Bench stood
conlirmed, thereby limiting the jurisdiction of the RDO to Ac.36.25
gts, the RDO had no authority to grant tenancy rights beyond that
extent. Thr: Joint Collector, sitting in appeal, also failed to examine
this vital issue. This Court, while exercising revisional jurisdiction
under Section 92 of the Tenancy Act, is empowered to pass orders
consistent with the Act, whether by way of confirmation, rescission,
or modification of the order under appeal or revision, as appears to be
just and by virtue of the powers vested under sub-section (2) of
A

1,42 cva&J
CRP No.5279n0rc &3arch
Section 89, this Ceurt is competent to modify the order of the RDO
dated 15.1O.2O13.
46. In ttre absence of cogent or unimpeachable evidence to the
contrar5r, and in the light of admitted failure to comply with the
procqdure under Section 19 of the Tenancy Act concerning surrender
of tenancy rights, this Court finds that the certilied entries in the
Tenancy Register; coupled with the RDO's report dated 27.02.1981,
are conclusive of the fact that the revision petitioners continued to be
in possession as protected tenants. The record further indicates that
these lands remained classified as agricultural lands as per the
entries maintained under the Telangana Land Revenue Act, L}LZ
Fasli. The provisions of the A.P. (Telangana Area) Record of Rights in
Land Regulation, 1358 Fasli, made applicablc across the State vide
Government Notifications dated 07.1O. 1953 and 24.08.1955,
mandated the preparation and finalization of Rccord of Rights for
each village. Notification No.53 dated 24.O8.1955 spccilically included
all lands covered by the Tenancy Acr, I95O. As per Rule 3 of the
Rules, tJre entries in Columns L to 19 of the Pahani Pafrika are
Record of Rights. These statutory records establish the tenancy rights
of the revision petitioners. The contention that tenancy rights were
surrendered in the year 1960, specificaily under File
Nos-A3/10805/6,4, A313749/6s, and A4/2ss6/66, is unfounded, as
a)

year 195,8, Lingamma had already divested her rights by executing
registererl sale deeds in favour of llyas Burni, and any surrender
made thereafter has no relevance.

48. Sri C-Hanumantha Rao, learned counsel who had earlier
represented Kastopa Corporation in the proceedings before the ULC
Authorit'y, is now appearing for the respondents/plot owners in these
revisions. The stand he presentl5r took in ,6e-s€ cases is inconsistent
with the stand taken by him before the Urc Authority. He now
disputes the very existence of tenants over the subject lands, despite
the entri,:s recorded in the tenanry register prepared. under Section
g7-A of t:re Tenancy Act. Having *g"rd to the submissions made by
the declar-ant as well as the learned counsel, it ca+ be safely held that
any allegr:d compromise or alienation of the lands prior to the vesting
date, particularly if done in violation of Section 33 of the Tenancy Act,
is invalid. Likewise, any sale deeds ocec"ufed without following the
procedure prescribed under Section 38;E or Section 19 o[ the
Tenancy Act are uoid ab initio.
49. Section 37A of Tenancy Act was inserted vide Amendment Act
No.lll of L1)56, dated 12.03.1956 conferring the rights on the tenants.
As such, any compromise or any alienation prior to the vesting datc
are deemerl to be in violation of Section 33 of the Act and the same
are void.
50. The subsequent G.O;Ms.No.183 dated 24.O3.2OOO issued
under thc IJrban Areas (Development) Act, Lg75, notifying the lands
as falling under 'residential use" ?Dtre, has no effect on the protected

148 cvBR J
CRPNo.5279n0rc & Batch
tenancy rights which had already vested. Section 1O4 of the Tenancy
Act has overriding effect over all other enactments. Unless there is a
notification under section 102(e) of the Tenanry Act, which is absent
in this case, the applicability of 'the'Tenanry Act continues. The
doctrine of lis pend"ens mandates that rights must be adjudicated
under the legal regime prevailing as on the date of commencement of
litigation, and subsequent enactments cannot defeat vested rights.
Furthermore, as held in Mir Sardor All a. Mqndal Reuenue Ofjicer,
r(escra's case (supra), mere inclusion of agricultural lands within
municipal limits or reclassif-rcation under other enactments cannot
extinguish the tenancy rights unless specilic stahrtory. notifications
are issued under the Tenancy Act. similarly, in the case of Kotalah
v. Propertg Assn. of the Bapdrt Cfuirches (supr{, the Hon'ble
Supreme Court reiterated that a protected tenant acquires ownership
upon issuance of notification under Section 38-E and cannot be
dispossessed illegally. It is settled law that when a person is
disputing the status of another person as that of the protected tenant
or deemed protected tenant, the former'is required to Ele necessary
application in that regard and invite an order of the authority on the
dispute rega-rding the status of tenancy in favour of another person.
' Admittedly, in the sale deeds said to have been executed in favour of
respondents by the vendors (Kastopa Corporation and the tenants), it
was stated that there are protected tent lts. Further, the vendor i.e,
t;

n
t49 CVB&J
CRP No.5279 f2O I 6 & Batch
Kastopzr Corporation also in written arguments admitted that there
are protected tenancy rights and the nature of land is agricultural
land and prayed to allow the revision petitions filed by the tenants
(As per tle Counter alfid,aait fited bA M/s.Kastopa Corporation uiz.,
Resporrclent No.39 in CRP No.5279/2OIq. The alleged sale deeds
executecl in favour of the respondents were denied by the petitioners
stating that same are fraudulent documents. [n the circumstances in
absence of any order of the competent authorit5r regarding denying
the right of protected tenant in relation to the subject lands by filing
appropri:r.te application, no fault can be found in declaring the
petitione::s as entitled for certificates under Section 38E/38-A.
51. Tht: claim that the purchasers under various sale deeds

were execrrted during the pendency of tenancy litigation, and are thus

permission from the District Collector under Telangana land

development charges or submission of layout plans to municipal
